All my faith in God, saying: Jesus. [unclear] the new [unclear] of the brothers does not come, but to see life.
And their sons [unclear] the earth and the planted things and the rivers and the soul and the waters and the images and the eminences and the discourse of sacred things.

*Of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Saint James the Apostle [unclear], Book Five of the Polygraphia: beginning of the chapter.*

Those things which, with the help of our Lord Jesus Christ, we have set forth, as best we could, in the four preceding books for the completion of our Polygraphia, reasonably require that in the fifth book we briefly treat the remaining precepts and methods of this instruction that are left over. We shall therefore give in it a sufficiently concise method by metathesis, that is, by the transposition of letters, by which a friend may write securely, safely, and most secretly to a friend whatever he wishes. We read that Caesar Augustus used this manner of writing in secret matters. This method of sending messages, although in many ways secure, is nevertheless always regarded as suspect by those who look upon it, because as soon as a transposition of letters is seen, anyone curious immediately suspects that something lies hidden beneath them. But the methods of this invention are almost infinite; from them we have marked out the following for the reader as a necessary introduction, reserving the rest to be searched out by his own ingenuity. First, therefore, we have put before him the standard table of this practice, to which we have added a second, reversed one. In these two tables the root and foundation of the aforementioned transposition are wholly laid down, upon which the structure of the whole edifice is placed. For once these two tables have been properly known and understood, no transposition of letters will be able to escape one who works diligently. To these we have joined the order of twenty-four alphabets. The use of these, as one wishes, consists in each individually, or, if you prefer, even in all of them. If you have secret dealings with many people, assign individual alphabets to each of your confidants, while you commit to memory what you have given to each one. Let the first alphabet be entrusted to the first person, the second to the second, the third to the third, and so on with the rest. But if, because of a very great number of affairs or of associates, the alphabets which we have marked out should not suffice for all things, or if some of them seem too open and obvious, we shall be able to introduce various new transpositions, whose number may be very great, while the method of the secret always remains perpetually hidden. Let us give an example of this matter: let B be a, f be b, l be c, p be d, t be e, z be f, c be g, m be h; and so let it be done consequently through the whole customary alphabet, so that between any chosen letter three lie idle and transposed. Afterwards four, then five, more or fewer, yet in such a way that the whole alphabet, as though revolved in a circle, comes together, and no letter at all is omitted, but each one is placed in its own order in the same sequence for another. In whatever way you write, that very method must be firmly entrusted either to writing or to memory, lest, having slipped through forgetfulness, you can no longer read what you yourself had written. For the transposition of letters can be varied in so many ways that, according to the estimate of human understanding, which, enclosed by temporal operation in a mortal body, does not properly grasp infinities, there will arise for a mortal an innumerable number. Hence whoever wishes to have more methods of writing by metathesis, apart from our instruction, if he is even a little ingenious and diligent, will easily achieve the effect in his work. For the mind of man can do very great things if it is aided by study; but art that despises the smallest things never reaches the greater. I know that there will be very many who will mock these studies of ours as vain and childish; to them we give this answer: that we shall be entirely content provided they please those at whose request they were composed by us. The straight table follows.

*Direct table of transposition.*

[table of letters and numerals] multiplied the same number of times, they make a little less than fourteen thousand.

*Reverse table of transposition.*

[table of letters and numerals] In this reverse table, you have just as many alphabets through the transposition of letters as in the preceding one, which we also set down as the direct table. For, as you see, under metathesis there are twenty-four toward the right. Likewise the same number toward the left; downward, twenty-four; and upward, ninety-eight. Multiplied four times twenty times, reduced in the form of a cross to the four sides, they make no fewer than one hundred and twelve alphabets.

The same table follows, expanded.

*First figure of the expansion of the direct table.*

[expanded table of letters] This is the first figure of the expansion of the direct table: five alphabets by metatheses, as is seen, with transposition of the letters. For in the first alphabet b is taken for a, c is taken for b, and so on consecutively, as is clearer to one who looks closely. In the second alphabet g is taken for a; in the third, d; in the fourth, e; in the fifth, f, as the placement of the order teaches.

The expansion is continued.

*Second figure of the expansion of the direct table.*

[expanded table of letter-pairs] In this second figure of the expansion of the direct table there are likewise contained five alphabets in number, from which by metathesis many others also can be made; their arrangement, for the sake of brevity, we have omitted. [unclear].

The expansion is continued.

All these things are sufficient as an example for those who cannot be content with fixed ones, so that they may have a method and form for discovering the rest, since they can be varied without number.

ii

*Johannes Trithemius, Abbot [unclear], to the reader, greeting.*

Since all the letters of the Greeks and Hebrews are numerical, I have often wondered that the inquisitive Latinity of the ancients did not also make its own letters serve the reckoning of numbers. The Hebrews, by doubling certain letters, established twenty-eight signs sufficient for every number. The Greeks, however, with like foresight relieved this lack of letters by the addition of four characters. Since we therefore have twenty-four letters, why do we not also make our own numerical, seeing that we can similarly find four doubled letters? Let us give four as an example, and all will be sufficient for number: final m and n; likewise closed v and open u. When we add to these the twenty-four usual letters, we have characters sufficient for all twenty-eight numbers. And it will be no matter of very great labor to commit this number to memory.

For this invention of ours will be able to be useful to many, since in number it comprehends many thousands in few letters, all of which at first glance, without any calculation, can be discerned and understood more clearly than light. Many who are less experienced in the art of calculation think that this cannot be done so easily by the figures of algorism, especially in large numbers. For as soon as you have looked with your eye upon any number, however great, you will be able both to understand it fully and to express it without circumlocution or division of speech. Since this is done so easily, it is fitting that we mark the letters themselves reduced into number. Written 16 [unclear] 1508.

*Order of numerical letters.*

| yw | 800000 | ozw | 6000000 | t2w | 40000000 |
| zw | 900000 | p2w | 7000000 | v2w | 50000000 |
| k2w | 1000000 | q2w | 80000oo | u2w | 60000ooo |
| l2w | 200000o | r2w | 9000oooo | x2w | 7000uooo |
| m2w | 3000000 | s2w | 1000oooo | y2w | 800ooooo |
| n2w | 4000ooo | s2w | 200ooooo | z2w | 900ooooo |
| n2w | 500oooo | S2w | 300ooooo | K22w | 100ooooooo | Behold, reader, you have the method by which you can adequately mark down and write every writable number with a few letters; their range, as you see, proceeds to infinity. But, as people once used to say in a proverb, “The weary ox plants his foot firmly”: whoever wishes there to be a limit to the preceding things, let him follow the ancient method.

So that those who do not know it may understand it more easily, it will not be tedious for me to give in what follows the form which Hunibald the Frank, historiographer of the Franks, and also Bede, Rabanus, the monks Helpericus, and many of the ancients used in chronicles and ecclesiastical computations, as follows.

*The ancient order of numerical letters*

| c̄ | 30000000 | c̄ | X | 100000000 |
| ---: | :---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---:|

| D | 50000000 | c̄ | XX | 20000000 | c̄ | lxxx | 80000000 ||

| dcccc | 9000 | c̄ | LX | 60 | c̄ | XLX | 10 || We have thus far set forth, by way of example, the ancient manner of the Franks of Germany in writing numbers with Latin letters, so that we might also provide knowledge of it to those who do not know it. But the fact that, in setting down the figures of algorism, we have departed from the common rule of computation was done not by mistake but for a reasonable cause, namely so that we should not be burdened by an excessive progression. There were also some who wrote larger numbers in this manner.

*The sixth book of the Polygraphia of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Saint James the Greater the Apostle at Würzburg, begins.*

Before every invention and use of letters, in the beginning of the world, when human beings were still rough in understanding and uncultivated in manners, under the governance of a succession shared among three, as certain persons think, of intelligences succeeding one another in order for one thousand sixty-three years, the influences of the planets were in the world, acting in their own way upon bodies on earth. The ancient investigators of this matter say that Saturn, from the beginning of the world, presided by his influence over the things sprouting and growing on earth, principally through the governance of his intelligence, which they called Orifiel, that is, the life-giving one of God, for three hundred fifty-four lunar years, likewise four months, and as many days and hours. These things we recount as poetic; we do not affirm them.

After Saturn, Venus, and after Venus, Jupiter is held to have assumed dominion. After Jupiter, however, Mercury, fourth in order as ruler, adorned the talents of human beings with various arts. But under the three intelligences mentioned, namely while Saturn, Venus, and Jupiter were ruling, they assert that human beings did not have the use or knowledge of letters. Hence whenever one person wished to announce some secret to another, he did so not by writings, since the function of letters was lacking, but symbolically, by certain likenesses of animals, trees, plants, or other sensible things, according as each had agreed among themselves.

The age of Saturn, as they say, gave human beings manners suited to the influence proper to it. For it made farmers, hunters, founders of cities, rough men, woodland-dwellers, gloomy men, simple men, uncultivated in life and manners, living in the flesh for a long time. After Orifiel of Saturn, as those who hold such opinions say, the intelligence Anael, mover of Venus, received the governance of the world by influence for three hundred fifty-four

[unclear], months, days, and four hours; under him human beings began to be more cultivated, to build houses more frequently, and cities, and also to invent various manual arts, namely weaving, wool-working, and many other things similar to these. They also began to sink more deeply into the pleasures of the flesh, to take beautiful wives, and, when many children had been begotten from them, they notably fell away from natural simplicity. They invented games and songs, began to play the lyre, and whatever they declare to pertain to the influence of Venus they devised rather eagerly, following too much the impulse of the flesh.

They named the third intelligence, that of Jupiter, Zachariel; taking up dominion over the world after Anael, it made men restless with desire for ruling, to engage in hunts, to make tents, and to adorn them with various coverings. While he held sway there came about, in accordance with the quality of Jupiter, who deprived his father of the kingdom, a great division between the good and the evil: the former calling upon God, the latter given over to the flesh and the world. Under that rule Adam, the first man, died, leaving to all posterity the testament of dying once.

The fourth mover, or angel of Mercury, Raphael, after Zachariel, pouring influence upon worldly affairs, began in the year of the world 1055 at the end of February, and lasted until the year 1410; under him the use of letters is said first to have been invented. At that time, however, the letters were, as they report, different from those which the world has today, devised in the form and likeness of trees, plants, and animals; yet afterward, with time, they were changed in many ways, and took on an easier manner of forming and a more cultivated ornament. For the fictions of the poets said that Mercury was the presiding deity of all literature, promising that great secrets of things lay hidden beneath fables.

After this, when the human race had multiplied and had spread everywhere over all the earth, the unity of letters did not long remain; rather, the division of bodies and minds far from one another found diverse and various modes of writing, concerning which there is a long narrative for us. Plato the philosopher, in the Phaedrus, relates that a certain philosopher named Theuth first invented letters and the use of writing, together with the reckoning of weights, numbers, and the game of dice, and taught Thamus, king of the Egyptians. But concerning the other ancient men who are said to have handed down special letters to almost each nation and people, letters which remain in use among them down to the present day, it is not my purpose on this occasion to set down or indicate the narrative.

For all our teachers know that Cadmus, son of Agenor, king of the Phoenicians, is said first to have brought sixteen letters into Greece: α, β, γ, δ, ε, η, ι, λ, μ, ν, ο, π, ρ, σ, τ, ϖ. To these Palamedes, at the time of the Trojan War, is reported to have added four: ξ, θ, φ, χ. And Pythagoras afterward added the letter v, so that there should be twenty-one. ζ, κ, and ω were subsequently invented by others. Jordanes, bishop of the Goths, gave letters proper to that people. When the unity of language had been confounded, the simplicity of characters too was varied in many ways.

Hence it came about that the use of letters, in the course of time, usurped such license for itself that not only individual peoples, but also individual men, fashioning new characters for letters at their own discretion, prepared hiding places for their mysteries. Yet every writing in an unusual character will not be free from suspicion among those who see it. Notwithstanding this, many kings, tyrants, and princes of old once devised for themselves a familiar mode of writing, separate from the common one, caring little for the suspicion of others, provided only that what they wanted to remain secret was not perceived. Moved by imitation of them, and desiring to bring this work of Polygraphy, as far as can be done, to a perfect conclusion, in this sixth book, which is also the last, I have resolved to call back from the dead certain characters of the ancients, which have long since passed out of mortal use, lest they perish altogether. And first indeed we shall set down the ancient mode of writing of the Northmen.

*On the alphabet and mode of writing of the Northmen, wonderful at the time when they were ravaging Gaul by their incursion.*

[unclear]

*All the letters of the Greeks and Palestinians are numerals,* as was said before, while of the letters of the Latins, which we Germans also use, only seven [unclear] into numbers [unclear].

are referred, namely c, d, i, l, m, v, and x. The Normans, however, together with the Asiatic Dacians, having once come forth from the Scandinavian islands, which are now called Gothic, roamed through Gaul for more than forty years and devastated it most grievously. In order to provide for their secret affairs, as Bede the English monk bears witness, they devised from the letters of the Greeks a new and elegant method of writing of this kind. For they adapted the numbers of the Greeks up to twenty-four, comprised in ten letters, to Latin characters, and prudently hid our letters under a foreign form, namely in the way in which we use numbers in place of letters: that is, when for a we put 1, for b 2, for c 3, for d 4, and so on successively to the end of the alphabet. It was indeed a fine and sufficiently safe invention, which neither a Greek nor a Latin will easily understand. For the whole alphabet is not found in it, but only nine letters and one numerical sign, namely q̄, which among them signifies six; and in this manner of writing it stands for f, the sixth letter of the Latins. Furthermore, paying regard to our German tongue in which we were born, we have placed w, double, at the end as the twenty-fourth letter of our nation, which we have formed according to the Greek number thus: kδ. We shall set out this script of the Northmen from the following order of Greek letters, in imitation of them.

A α | 1 | i | [unclear] | 9 | T ζ | 17
B β | 2 | k | t | 10 | Γ υ | 18
C γ | 3 | l | τ | 11 | δ κ | 19
D ∂ | 4 | m | π | 12 | v λ | 20
E ε | 5 | n | ψ | 13 | X κ̄ | 21
F φ | 6 | o | ω | 14 | Y κρ | 22
G g | 7 | P | q | 15 | Z κυ | 23
H h | 8 | r | θ | 16
I i | 9 | s | ϖ | 17 | kɔ | 24 They represent the Latin letters, and they will be readable neither by a Greek nor by a Latin who has not first acquired knowledge of this device. This method of writing can also be varied in many ways, when the letters are changed by transposition of the number. This can be done in Greek letters in full, according to the chosen will of each industrious writer. Nor will the manner of forming these characters be very difficult, especially for one who has become somewhat practiced. The characters written above also suit the German language sufficiently well, since with them we can denote everything that occurs to us, in whatever speech we may be skilled. Whenever iota is placed by itself, so that it may be known to denote the letter k, the tenth of the alphabet, thus ĩ, it is marked above.

*On the Alphabet and Method of Writing of the Ancient Franks.*

The opinion of very many holds that the Germans once had letters from the Greeks, and they steadfastly affirm that they used the Greek language especially in their sacred rites. For they say that a great correspondence exists between the Greek language and our own, although the long interval of time has utterly extinguished that agreement. That most learned man, once my teacher, Johannes Camerarius Dalburgius, most reverend bishop of the church of Worms, had collected several thousand Greek words which signify the same thing in both languages, Greek and German. And Caesar testifies in the Commentaries that the ancient Druids, priests of the sacred rites in Gaul, used the Greek language, especially in sacred matters. But when the Romans were laying waste the world, they at length violently imposed the use of their letters, together with their dominion, upon many nations. Finally the remnants of the Trojans, who were afterward called Franks, in the year before the incarnation of the Lord four hundred in the [unclear] fortieth [year], having gone out from the borders of the Scythians, with King Marcomer providing leadership, they came into Germany, and from their Saxon brothers received settlements at the mouths of the Rhine. Thereafter, waging war almost continuously with the Romans and Gauls for nine hundred and fifty years, as Hunibald is the authority, they finally brought almost all Europe under their empire or kingdom. Yet they did not abolish the letters of the Romans once they had received them, but rather, mingled among the Germans and Gauls, they learned their language, and preserved their letters and form of writing. Wasthald, a writer of the histories of this people, wrote in his native tongue books of the deeds of kings, dukes, and princes over almost seven hundred and fifty-eight years, with the following characters appended, as Hunibald says; he mentions them in his volume, which he published in eighteen books on the origin of the Franks.

Λα θε ϖν ζη υζ

Εζ Ψμ στ ωω Unless the fault of the scribe has deceived me, I have represented Wasthald’s alphabet suitably, as I think. In it some characters differ entirely from the Greek, while some also seem partly to resemble them. Yet I do not know what to judge: whoever their inventor was, although he did not keep the form of the Greek letters, he nevertheless observed the order of the letters. The manuscript, crumbling from excessive age, could scarcely be read; hence I fear that I have rather been deceived by some interpolator than found Hunibald’s true and certain meaning.

* * *
* * *

But let us pass on to what remains, since if Hunibald’s statement is true, it will have been well written by us; but if it is someone’s fiction, it will neither harm us nor do injury to the reader. There follows another fictitious alphabet found in Hunibald’s manuscript, to which this was inscribed:

*The Alphabet of the Franks, which Doracus invented.*

Τ d E k A p η t X x
Π c M l W ph b th X z Whoever wishes to use the characters written above should know that they must be written below the lines in the manner of the Greeks, not above as the Latins do. Likewise it must be observed that, of double letters, the first are written at the beginning and in the middle of words, but the second always at the end.

*From the grammars of Otfrid, monk of Wissembourg.*

We read that Charles, king of the Franks, surnamed the Great, taking pity on the barbarous condition of his nation, with Nanno, Theobald, Albinus, and Berenger as helpers, attempted to give rule to the German language. And although, as Turpin bears witness, he had begun so arduous a work, he did not complete it, being hindered by frequent incursions of wars; but, forestalled by death, he left it unfinished, nor did another arise after him who would recall care for this matter to mind. Otfrid, however, the already mentioned monk, formerly hearer and disciple of Rabanus of Fulda, under Louis the Pious, son of that Charles, having mastered that grammar, however imperfectly, wrote many things in our German language, which seem to sound forth the admirable gravity of a regular instruction. For he worked out many poems according to that same rule, in heroic and also elegiac meter; moreover he composed some things also in prose to Louis, king of Germany, son of Louis the Pious and brother of Emperor Lothar; also to Otgar, archbishop of Mainz, to the monks of Saint Gall, and to others. Having obtained fragments of this grammar, I too have extracted the present characters from many, lest they should perish entirely. It is established, with Otfrid as witness, that Charles devised several alphabets of his own, which his very extensive kingdom might use securely in secrets with individual prefects. From these, with Otfrid supplying the letters, we commit these few things.

 a b c d e f g h i k l m n o p q r s t u v x y z Charles the Great, king and most Christian emperor, fought for no less than thirty years with the Saxons, whom at last, overcoming them by the sword, he converted to the Christian faith. But fearing lest they should again apostatize from the faith, as they had done many times, he appointed certain secret inspectors, to whom he granted judicial power, who, traveling through all Saxony, were to inquire secretly and diligently concerning the faith and morals of the people; and whomever they found to have apostatized from the faith, robbers, adulterers, blasphemers, contemners of the church or of its priests and commands, or those [unclear] by notorious crimes the Christian disturbing the public [peace], or recalling or soliciting the people to paganism, they should without delay, by imperial and royal authority, hang them with a noose with impunity, or otherwise kill them as they could. And so that this institution might remain perpetually unshaken, he gave the same men the power of substituting also others, fit men under certain conditions, who, enjoying the aforesaid faculty, might with impunity exercise the office of inquiry and of death against the guilty. Finally he prescribed for them secret laws and hidden signs, together with a form of oath, by which they might proceed justly in judging and punishing, and, known mutually to themselves, might remain hidden from others, and might preserve perpetually in Saxon land the necessary judgment in greater secrecy. They also used certain alphabets among themselves for a time; these, however, at length fell entirely out of use. Yet the office of this sort of inquisition remains down to the present time, whose ministers are commonly called [felmeri?].

Yet continued practice in writing will be able, by an easy correction, to amend this misshapen crookedness, and by frequent use to make it pleasing by habit. For no one, however learned or educated he may be, is a good writer in any language unless by long habit he has transformed the practice of writing into an art. Another also follows.

The first is that he form characters of easy drawing; and the simpler they are, the better they will be. The second is that he arrange the individual letters so that, when joined together into words, they present a beautiful and elegant appearance. I add a third, not promised: if you wish the writing to remain secret, make some letters of the alphabet double, after the manner of the Hebrews.

f ♦ f ♦ m ♦ ♦ ♦ ♦ ♧ ♨ ♩ Alchemy has many domestic attendants, who guard their mistress with perpetual vigilance and put themselves forward under her name, so that they may preserve her forever untouched from intercourse with so many importunate lovers. Vanity, fraud, deceitfulness, deception, sophisticating, greed, lying confidence, foolishness, want, poverty, despair, flight, outlawry, and beggary are the handmaids of alchemy; while pretending that their mistress is loved, they guard her inviolate, and gladly prostitute themselves to the moneyed, avaricious, greedy, and silly seekers of her.

R

*On the signs and wonderful, but excessively laborious, method of writing of Marcus Tullius Cicero, and afterwards of Saint Cyprian, bishop and martyr.*

Marcus Tullius Cicero, the eloquent orator of the Romans, wrote a book of signs of no small size. Saint Cyprian, bishop of Carthage and martyr, enlarged it with many signs and words, adding terms necessary for Christian uses, so that the work itself became useful not only to pagans, but much more also to the faithful.

The manuscript is rare; I have found it only once, and bought it at a cheap price. For when, in the year of the Lord's nativity 1496, I was examining many libraries out of love for books, I found the aforesaid manuscript in a certain monastery of our order, neglected because of its extreme age, cast away under dust and contempt. I asked the abbot, a doctor of law, at what price he valued it. He answered: he would prefer to it the little works of Saint Anselm, recently printed. I went to the booksellers, since the matter happened in a metropolitan city, and bought the requested little works of Anselm for one sixth of a florin; I gave them to the abbot, with the monks rejoicing, and freed the manuscript, which had already nearly been brought to destruction. For they had resolved, out of love for the parchment, that it should immediately be scraped.

Almost two years after this, I rode to Strasbourg on business of my order, and, being admitted by Johannes Keisersberg, the distinguished preacher of the place, into the library of the cathedral church, I found a psalter written wholly in the same signs of Tullius and Cyprian, and most handsomely written with golden capitals. But an inscription had been placed on the outside by someone ignorant of the mystery, as follows: “Psalter in the Armenian language.” I brought in a scholar, showed the falsity, and advised that it should be rewritten thus: “Psalter written in Ciceronian signs.” Whether he did so or not I am uncertain, because I did not afterwards return to that library. So that you may see, reader, the form of Cicero's notation, we have decided to append a few signs.

*How, also by numbers and in many other various ways, we can safely write any secrets whatever.*

These things are childish, I admit, since each person, according to his own capacity and judgment, can devise more and more elegant alphabets than we have recorded in this volume. Therefore we knowingly omit many things which, for a similar reason, are childish and we judge [it] to be of little usefulness. We fear that someday we shall be ashamed to have applied our mind to these trifles, while more useful matters were left aside. But, I pray, the readers will forgive me; for my own inclination indeed drew me back from these childish studies, not to say [unclear], vain ones, but the insistence of certain friends compelled me to write them, to whom I neither could nor ought to deny this without injury to the bond contracted. Nevertheless, led by their entreaty, I also had to join the following little chapter to the preceding ones, and to place numbers in place of letters. The ancients established various and multiple modes of writing by means of figures of numbers, by which, for subtle discovery, they safely transmitted their secrets to one another. Therefore, persuaded by the prayers of friends, we have placed at the end of this sixth book a few methods out of many, by which a way to knowledge of others may lie open to those who do not know them. For if anyone takes pleasure in knowing these things, he will easily be able, once these are known, to climb to higher things, and to vary the discovered methods in manifold alternation according to his own judgment. As a beginning for this matter we have placed a table beneath the figure, which, varied [unclear], will contain in itself an alphabet in the form of a cross, ninety-six.

*The numerical circle in place of letters.*

We have not expanded the orchemata, for a reason that moved us: because, since with its progression it ranges farther afield, the immense labor has frightened the mind. We have also deliberately passed over unity, not assigning it a place, since we judge it rash to set it in order before our inventions. For from those things which we have already shown by way of example, one who is fit in understanding will easily attain greater things. All things serve secrets.
