r iiij The sixth and last book of our *Polygraphy* we have, by God’s mercy, brought to its close. In it, concluding our discourse with the wandering stars, we warn the reader in certain points. First, that as often as he uses an alphabet, he should prudently add the character or mark of some sign or planet, for the sake of a twofold usefulness. For by this means excessive labor is removed from the friendly reader, and the mystery will remain secret, since anyone ignorant of the craft, seeing a table written with numbers and marked beforehand with the characters of signs or planets, will think that it pertains to the method of astronomy, and will suspect that no mystery at all lies hidden. For this reason we have not begun [unclear] with the official letters, but from a distance, lest we give anyone occasion for suspicion. Nevertheless prudence must be used by writers, since an astute reader who is touched by suspicion of something hidden, if he detects the function of one number, will easily compute the whole alphabet.

Second, let anyone who desires to be a shrewd practitioner of this matter remember that whenever in any word a letter is doubled, as often happens, it should be written only once and marked above with a point. For example, I wish to write “Ioannes” from the alphabet of Aries: I do not duplicate 18, which is n, but write thus, 14. 19. 6. 18. 10. 23., “ioanes,” “abas,” so that every sign of suspicion may be removed. In similar cases similar caution must be exercised; for unless the practitioner is endowed with skill, and knows that greater things are to be found from small ones, he will not be a fit person to whom these very small matters of ours ought to be revealed. Not everything is to be communicated indiscriminately to everyone, especially things whose use can be turned to good and to evil. Moved reasonably by this consideration, we have plainly handed down in these six books of the *Polygraphy* many things to those who wish to know them, but we have allowed far more secrets to lie hidden beneath things manifest. Finally, we have written a key that lays open the whole work, showing how each metaphor is to be understood; yet by the counsel of certain men we have ordered it to remain hidden, so that studious persons may not lack material for exercising their memory, and that the cause of knowing it may be taken away from the wicked, who are not willing to use it well. In each passage many mysteries lie hidden, and the greater the things you discover from these, the more ample are those that remain to be found.

A way is prepared for beginners; for those who advance in it, it will not be difficult, in the course of time, to discover many rare and very great things. Here he who did not know how will learn to write Latin, and prudently to wrap all the mysteries of his will in a foreign language. But so that the ignorant man may be able to understand not only his own mysteries which he wraps up, but also open speech of which he has until now always been ignorant, the matter is not one of great labor, if the paraphrast has fully followed our intention in this volume. For beneath the bark lie the kernels of our *Steganography*, buried by just reason, lest they lie open to unworthy babblers. Finally it was worth the trouble that one work should not prejudice the other, but that its own right should be distributed to each unimpaired.

We, weary after a long voyage, here cast anchor in the sea under the name of JESUS CHRIST, the savior of the faithful, on the twenty-first day of the month of March, in the year of the Lord’s nativity 1508, in the eleventh indiction of the Romans, at [Paeapolis?], a city of the eastern Franks, which they call Würzburg, Herbipolis.

*The end of the sixth book of the Polygraphy of Johannes Trithemius, abbot at St. James in Würzburg, formerly of Sponheim, of the order of the divine father Benedict, is here.*

*Printed at the cost and expense of the most upright bookseller Johannes Haselberg of Aia, of the diocese of Constance, in the year 1518, in the month of July.*

[unclear]

*The Key of the Polygraphy of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of St. James at Würzburg, formerly of Sponheim, of the Order of Saint Benedict, of the observance of the Bursfelde fathers.*

With privilege that it is not to be printed or sold by another for ten years, under penalty as stated in the letters.

Johannes Trithemius 83

*Preface to the explanation of the whole work of the Polygraphy of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Saint James of Würzburg.*

I trust that no one who reads these things will judge me either arrogant or superfluous because I myself have written a commentary on the Polygraphy recently composed by me, especially if he has been able to weigh precisely what reason induced us to speak obscurely. For whatever can do harm when made public had reasonably to be concealed in mystical language. But the subject that we treat in the Polygraphy is, for the advantage of the commonwealth among good men, very good, useful, and honorable; and for that reason, persuaded by a reasonable cause, we wrote it, so that by its service we might make good men safe and secure from the investigation of the wicked when they communicate their secrets to one another. But lest an opportunity be offered to the wicked and perverse, who even more frequently misuse good inventions, to deceive the simpler and those less cautious in judging matters, we handed down in it mystical or hidden methods of writing, and covered the truth lying hidden in the marrow with certain coverings of figures. Yet we do not at all doubt that any industrious man, eager with desire for these things, will easily grasp everything, if he applies even a modest persistence in searching them out. For the things I have handed down are neither so difficult nor enveloped in such great mysteries that they cannot be wholly penetrated by a studious man. For everywhere we have so tempered the riddles of our words that they may easily be accessible to learned and studious men, and nevertheless remain inaccessible to sluggish children of darkness. But after it pleased me to offer the very work of the Polygraphy, dedicated, to the most serene and most unconquered Caesar of the Romans, Maximilian, ever august emperor of the world, I thought it would be worthwhile if, with a few brief explanations, I should make it clear open, lest the mind of his sacred majesty, which is daily stretched by many affairs and cares, be drawn back from understanding it by the obscurity of the mysteries. For unless my opinion deceives me, the work will be the more pleasing and useful to his majesty the shorter and clearer it is. First, therefore, with the Lord’s granting, we shall briefly explain the meaning of our proposal, which in the prologue is contained wrapped in various enigmas; then, through the individual books of the Polygraphy, we shall succinctly unfold, according to the powers of our talent, the methods of working, the rules, cautions, and differences, in the order in which they stand as necessary. All these things, so that they may more quickly become accessible to those who desire to know, we shall show one by one through examples, and we shall be as brief as the reasoning of the matter permits.

*Key to the Explanation of the Polygraphy of Johannes Trithemius, [Paeapolitan?] Abbot.*

*Exposition on the Prologue.*

Our whole intention in the entire work of the Polygraphy turns upon this proposition: that we should hand down certain methods of writing secretly, by which kings, princes, nobles and commoners, priests and laymen, the learned together with the unlearned, may secretly, safely, and without any suspicion of a hidden secret, communicate to one another, and to any friends, even those far distant from one another, by the service of letters, whatever conception of their minds they need, as often as there is need. And lest we seem to have presumed to be either the first or the only ones to do this, we give notice beforehand that many of the ancients long ago, especially tyrants, princes, and kings, moved by a similar consideration, devised for themselves familiar and various methods and canons of writing, by which they themselves attempted in particular what we have promised universally in our Polygraphy. From their number we first introduced, for the sake of the proposed example, Caesar Augustus; take his secret method of writing as thus [unclear].

*Mystery.* He changed the breathings of conceived speech, with [unclear] excluded, into obscure things; their barbarousness, resisting his wishes, he makes obedient to his nod by a certain fine invention, the scheme of transposition.

*Explanation.* That is: the great Caesar Augustus, in order to write secretly and safely what he wished, changed the letters of the alphabet by varied transposition, so that he put b for a, c for b, and so consequently for each. And so that the writing might remain more twisted and more secret, at his own discretion he transposed the letters breadthwise and lengthwise, at any distance of order, number, and place.

*Mystery.* And this receptive order, as though perpetual, subject to the will and to the reckoning of time, although it preserves messengers safe on the road from the attack of the [Bacuceans?], nevertheless, since it has no regulated bounds, makes all who encounter it anxious with suspicion of a hidden thing.

*Explanation.* Although no one can easily grasp with the understanding the secret mystery lying hidden under the transposition of letters, nevertheless this writing makes those into whose hands it comes suspicious about the hiding place of the thing enclosed.

*Mystery.* Archimedes, that Syracusan, easily the foremost of mathematicians, a white bird suited to his devices, bare through head and feet, etc.

*Explanation.* Archimedes had two little rods of one length and thickness planed round or square; one of them he kept for himself, the other he entrusted to his absent friend. And as often as one wished to write something more secret to the other, he wound the white bird, that is, a strip of papyrus of small width but of twice the rod’s length, around the wood and fastened it with wax below and above. Afterwards he wrote on the strip, along the sides, continuously upon what had been artfully joined together into a mass, whatever he wished; then he loosened the strip from the rod, and at once so great a distance appeared between the letters that not even one word cohered with another. He turned the strip when he wished; on the other side he wrote likewise. Nor was there anyone ignorant of this device who could have discovered the secret. But the man who had the other rod, when he had received the strip, attached it and restored the letters to their former order.

*Mystery.* Marcus Tullius Cicero, the eloquent orator of the Romans, using art with talent, changed the minute parts of phrase into signs, etc.

*Explanation.* He wrote a book of signs for his son, which the blessed martyr Cyprian afterwards enlarged for the use of Christians, namely in the form of a dictionary.

where, in due order, the characters or signs are first set down, and after them the words designated by those same characters, so that each word is signified by the sign placed before it. With these signs or characters we once saw an entire Latin psalter, beautifully written, in the library of the greater church of Strasbourg. Some ignorant judge of such matters had put this title on the outside: “A Psalter in the Armenian language.” But he was mistaken, since it should rather have been written thus: “A Latin psalter written in Tullian characters.” For each character represents one whole Latin word. Nor is my judgment deceived, since I possess the very dictionary of Cicero, corrected by Saint Cyprian, written complete and uncorrupted in an ancient volume, which I have seen nowhere else. So great, indeed, is the abundance of signs or characters, with the words assigned to them, that they are entirely sufficient for writing in the Latin tongue whatever you wish.

*Mystery.* Faramundus, the forty-third from Marcomer, king of the Franks, when in the year of the Lord’s nativity 424 he entered the borders of Gaul [unclear] and powerfully, etc.

*Explanation.* The kings of the Franks, from the time when, in the year 430 before the birth of Christ, they left the Scythian borders with their people and settled at the mouths of the Rhine, where Guelders now is, waged continual war with the Gauls for almost nine hundred years, and at last violently made them subject to themselves, as we have written in the first volume of our Annals. Of these kings Faramundus, the aforementioned king, in order to send messengers safely through the kingdom, devised new letters proper to himself, which no one could know without his voluntary instruction. Nor did he use one alphabet only, nor a few, but many new alphabets.

*Mystery.* Clodius Crinitus, son of Faramundus and afterward king of the Franks, who subjected all Gaul to himself after the Romans, whom he found there, had been destroyed, used his father’s inventions in secret writings.

*Explanation.* This king fashioned alphabets of his own, new ones, and handed one to each of his dukes, while he alone retained with himself the knowledge of them all.

*Mystery.* Charles, surnamed the Great, the sixty-third king of the Franks, who was the first of the Franks to wear the Roman diadem of empire, etc.

*Explanation.* This man, the elder son of King Pippin, is read to have been born in Brabant, in the county of Namur, bordering on Liege, although someone asserts that he was born in the village of Ingelheim not far from Mainz. He too discovered various modes of writing, and, with Nanno the grammarian supplying assistance, devised different alphabets, by which he communicated secrets to the individual prefects of the kingdom in a singular fashion.

*Mystery.* The Northmen, who long ago came out from Gothia and its borders, when they were devastating Gaul with a cruel invasion, having been summoned from Greece, prepared a safe hiding place for [unclear] with owls for secrets, etc.

*Explanation.* The Northmen, ravaging Gaul, as Bede the monk in England bears witness, in order that they might safely write their secrets among themselves, set Greek numerical letters under the Latin letters, from a, the first, up to the twenty-fourth point, such as w, or gu, according to the custom of the Gauls. This mode of writing is altogether very beautiful: in it all the letters are Greek, and according to numerical order they designate Latin letters in Pelasgian figures, as a, 1; b, 2; c, 3; d, 4; e, 5; f, 6; g, 7; and so successively up to w, which is 24. Neither a Greek nor a Latin will understand this writing, unless he has first learned the method by diligence and art.

*Mystery.* Our Bede, a monk in England, ignorant of the deeds of the Northmen, described many old inventions similar to these, by which they in turn made a clear mystery obscure. He himself [wrote] *Artrology*, etc.

*Explanation.* This Bede, the monk, in his little book on the inventions of the ancients, reviewed many and various noteworthy methods of writing secretly, by which he safely announced his secrets to friends, and by which those present spoke without voice. He was the first of all to commit artrology and dactylology to letters; by their use one person may very safely reveal to another whatever secret he wishes, which none of those standing by will notice, and much less understand. Bede himself taught that this could be done in many of these and other ways. Since we have written nothing about it in the *Polygraphia*, we rightly decline to say more of it here too. We long ago assigned this very matter, in its own place, to the *Steganographia*, which we do not yet intend to publish publicly. And who could set forth all the methods of secret writing used by the ancients? For some, in the service of the art, had familiar [spirits] set over owls, the Philophoti; others had the Misophoti; others summoned both into practice by art. Again, others, having been made known, transposed the letters of the common alphabet; others, under the guise of barbarians, fashioned their own characters for themselves. Again [unclear].

Others used the service of visible letters, writing with red lead or with common ink; but others formed invisible characters at their own discretion and command. The methods of visible characters for secret writing are exceedingly many and altogether innumerable, and to make mention of them all is neither our wish nor within our power. But of invisible characters there are three kinds more commonly used among mortals today. The first is called dermatic, by which writing is made on the back of messengers, visible only with liquids; no one can penetrate it who does not know the secret of the art. The second is called hyphasmatic, by which something is written on cloth with certain substances; when dry it cannot be seen at all until, moistened in cold water, it is perceived. The third kind is called alleotic; it is manifold and varied, both in form and in material, and has almost innumerable methods of writing secretly. Its writing is made on paper or without paper, with [unclear] and [unclear], with [unclear], [unclear], and other diverse things. Likewise letters are written in various ways and hidden also in places, such as under [unclear], under [unclear], in [unclear], in [unclear], [unclear], in [unclear], in [unclear], and in other threads almost infinite in number. But because none of these things serves our present purpose on this occasion, we must therefore hasten to the matters that reason requires. There is also another kind, dactylethron, not indeed of writing, but by signs, by which in most subtle ways one can communicate to another the conception of one’s mind. This too is varied by so many distinctions that there is no one in the world who could enumerate all the forms of so great a mystery. By the principal method, and by many others deriving from it, the practitioner, sitting at table or in any other place whatever, will be able safely, most securely, and without anyone’s suspicion, to communicate whatever you wish to one who knows the art. Finally, how you may by letters declare the secret of your mind to an absent person, even without the knowledge of the very man who performs the office of messenger, remains assigned to the discoveries of steganography. For indeed, since kings and princes encounter many affairs of which they do not at times trust that knowledge can be given with sufficient safety by ordinary letters to their deputies far away, it will be necessary for each of them to have certain special methods of writing and sending messages, by which they may most safely make known their secrets to friends, especially in these times, in which, as license to sin prevails, no safe trust is found in the world.

Now the messengers of princes are seized everywhere by savage robbers, despoiled, beaten, and very often killed; nor do wicked plunderers spare even the letters of kings, sealed and closed with rings. Therefore it would be necessary for your commonwealth, O most glorious Caesar Maximilian, that you put on the skin of Hercules and brandish the club in your hands, and purge the world for yourself, by God, of these savage monsters. He who disturbs the peace of the kingdom shows himself to be the enemy of the king. These are the robbers who beset the roads of travelers; they seize what belongs to others. The earth is full of them today, and nowhere is the road safe for those who walk.

*Brief Explanation of Those Things Which Seem Rather Obscure in the First Book of the Polygraphy*

Although nothing obscure is contained in the first book of the Polygraphy, nevertheless, lest its composition perhaps seem profound to the simpler, we shall briefly explain the things that are thought more difficult. We have distributed the words of the Latin language in order, more than nine thousand of them, under almost four hundred alphabets, in such a serial way that if from each alphabet only one after another has been taken from the beginning to the end, they will cohere with one another and will make a fitting Latin sentence. For it will be a suitable method of writing most secretly whatever we wish, since each word represents the letter placed before it at the head. And let it not trouble you, reader, that the multitude of words in the open contains a brief sense in secret, since there lies hidden in this childish arrangement of ours another, higher method of writing whatever you wish more broadly and much more secretly, which public reason has forbidden us to disclose to all. But whoever is endowed with intelligence and applies the necessary powers and resources to the inquiry will find subtler things among the hidden teachings in these matters, and will understand things wonderfully unknown before and plainly more wonderful. Nevertheless, this suitable method of writing will suffice for all things, since you can repeat it as often as you wish, and by frequent repetition the narrative is varied. For if, as we have said, from the first alphabet you take one word, and from the second likewise one, and so successively one from each, and also one from the interposed words outside the ordered line, until the period or the end of the book, and a sufficiently fitting Latin discourse will arise for you. But one must take care that at one time, all at once, no more than one word be taken from one alphabet, lest the writer, through negligence or excessive haste, pass over some alphabet and confuse the order of the narration. By “day” understand the open narration, but by “night” the hidden, concealed one, which is not made too long but brief. The paradigms that follow in the text, concerning the invisible soul and the body that is seen, pertain to both narrations, the hidden and the manifest. “As often as a leaf is moved by the wind,” and the rest: we advise that you count the secret narration in relation to the open one in such a way that both end together at the close of each period. But if anything remains over from the secret matter, let it be extended with such moderation that both narrations end at one point, lest confusion of the eclipse be produced. The other things that follow in the first lesson of the explanation of the Polygraphy are all implicitly referred to what we have said.

It should be noted, however, that under the order of the alphabets no word is empty, but each represents the letter set before it at the head. So that the reader may not lack perfect knowledge of this matter, let us explain by an example. Let us suppose the case that a wicked man seeks through you to be advanced with some friend of yours; but you, in order to cut off his importunate entreaties and yet not advance an unworthy man, decide to write more secretly to your friend through the man who is asking, so that he may not understand: “Beware of this man, because he is a thief, worthless, most wicked.” Go to the first alphabet; write the word that signifies C. In the second, “clemens,” which gives A; in the third, “discernens,” which represents V; in the fourth, “mundana,” signifying E. Now in the hidden narration you have: “Beware.” Proceed successively also through the other alphabets, and a discourse like this will emerge: “Conditor clemens discernens omnibus sanctis suis in caelis, amen. Mansuetudo gloriosissimi principis a sanctis ubique reverenter rememoranda, optimis praeceptis nos abiectissimos dormientes hortatur praesentia propter venerationem directoris bonorum derelinquere, gloriam perpetuae quietis omni affectu [unclear] expectare. Eruditione igitur [unclear] derisores accipite, [unclear] ad felicissima tripudia archangelorum remuneratoris orthodoxorum valeatis ingredi felicissime decori. Refugite igitur a spurcissimis honoribus huius peregrinationis, cum sitis ad iucunditates benigni salvatoris saeculorum beatissimas sanctissime ingressuri.”

In this public narration written above there is contained in secret this hidden sentence, namely: “Beware of this man, because he is a thief and worthless, most wicked; but be silent, I pray.” One must proceed in the same order in all other cases whatsoever, and begin from the head as often as necessary, until you have enclosed the hidden narration of your mystery in the manifest one. But the words that are placed outside the alphabetical order designate no letter, nor do they serve the hidden narration; they have been inserted because of a lack of synonyms, so that the sequence of the Latin discourse might be completed. For we could not always have so many words of the same meaning, especially among pronouns, conjunctions, prepositions, and interjections, that we could have arranged an alphabet in place of the twenty-four roaming letters, as is clearly evident after the eighth alphabet, where we placed two words outside the order, lest we leave the discourse defective. For thus we put: “Cum omnibus”; and thus: “Cum universis.” Of these the writer may take the two he wishes, so that the Latin discourse may be completed. Let him do likewise in other cases, whenever he finds similar ones. We have adopted the double w because of our native speech, since in our German language we often need it. For whoever is ignorant of the Latin tongue may write his hidden secret in German under a Latin discourse without any difficulty.

*Now follow the rules or cautions to be observed in the operation of this book.*

The first rule or caution warns you that the secret of your mind which you wish to announce to an absent friend, one who knows the art and has the book of Polygraphy, you should write as carefully as you can on a sheet, in Latin or German, or in whatever other language you wish that can be comprised in Roman letters. Then place the paper so written before you, so that for each individual letter of the secret you may be able to fit a corresponding word suitably. For, as we have shown, in this practice of writing a whole word is taken. We gave an example a little earlier in what was set out above.

*II. The second precaution* instructs you, as has been said, not to take from any alphabet more than one word at a time in one narrative of public Latin discourse; otherwise you would confuse the open meaning and would entirely draw away from the understanding of the mystery the friend to whom you had written.

*III. Third,* one must take the utmost care not to skip through negligence any alphabet from which you do not take one word suited to the secret. Likewise one must provide that, whenever one or more words occur outside the series of the alphabet, you take the whole upper line, or the lower one, not both.

*IV. Fourth,* care must be taken that whenever the speech of the open discourse has been brought to a complete sentence, the next sentence should take its beginning from a larger, thickened letter, according to the form of the preceding example, in which, according to the sense of the Latin speech, we have taken large letters at the beginning and small letters in the rest, in the common manner. When the secret of your mind is lengthy, so that it exceeds the narrative of the open discourse, if much remains you should begin again from the beginning; if little remains, let the discourse be brought back at will from any place. For no one even slightly skilled in this art will be unable to know where you have begun again. Nor is there need to fear the harmony of the discourse, since the manifest narrative will always be varied with the hidden one; as often as one begins from the beginning, a new appearance of the speech will follow. The continuation can also be made from the end of the first book through the second.

*V. Fifth,* we advise that whenever you wish to write on a difficult matter by this paper, prepare for yourself a small booklet in which you write your secret mystery in manifest speech. Whoever sees or reads this booklet will think that no secret lies hidden, nor, even if he has suspicion, will he be able to discover any of the things that you write.

*VI. Sixth,* it should be noted: if, throughout the whole *Polygraphia*, you transpose the order of the words in each alphabet by changing it through metathesis, and write two volumes according to it, one for yourself and one for any friend, you will write between yourselves with the greatest security whatever you wish, and there lives no one on earth who can penetrate your secrets. This transposition, however, can be made in infinite ways.

*VII. Seventh,* we warn you beforehand that one must write polygraphically in one way to a man ignorant of Latin speech, and in another way to one skilled in it. For the former, all words must be written whole, without abbreviation, distinctly, completely, beautifully, and entirely legibly, without partition, without division, and without confusion, lest, being deceived, he think one to be two, or suppose two to be one. The things that are set down must be attended to.

*VIII. Eighth,* one must provide that the person to whom you write mystical things by this art should have a book exactly like yours in every respect; otherwise you would write vainly and fruitlessly to one who does not understand.

*IX. Ninth,* if you wish this art to remain forever secret and hidden, rewrite the *Polygraphia* anew, but do not set the letters of the alphabet before the words, and no one will easily suspect the method of the secret. But so that no difficulty may occur for the writer and the reader of the mystery, let the letters of the alphabet be written on a small sheet in the order corresponding to the words, and let a movable [unclear] be fitted to their headings. The letters can also be engraved on a copper plate, so that they may be moved from one order to another.

*X. Tenth,* it must be known that although we do not use the Pythagorean y in German and Latin words, nevertheless we have admitted it on account of the Greek language, so that we may have a doubled twelvefold set. Likewise we have adopted the doubled w as the twenty-fourth letter, which is altogether necessary for us in the German language.

*XI. Eleventh,* let the person who receives letters written from the *Polygraphia* to be read know that understanding is needed in the reading. And therefore, whenever he receives a paper or booklet written polygraphically, let him go to a secret place and, with the [book?] of the *Polygraphia* opened, consider the written words, and very diligently note with chalk on the table, or with ink on paper, what letter each one represents. And when he has gone through the whole letter in that way, let him compose the letters into words, and he will easily understand what has lain hidden in secret.

*XII. Twelfth,* let kings and princes note, who have many men in different places, [unclear] it is not safe. Therefore, so that they may have for each individual person their own secret manner of writing, let them change the order of the words as many times as [unclear].

they wished to have separately. And thus they themselves will possess all the modes of writing, and no one of their subjects will understand another’s mode. For there are infinite variable modes of writing more secretly in this art.

XIII. Thirteenth, it should be noted that, in order to stop the mouths of those who speak unjust things, we show that the truth of this new invention of ours is possible, placing the words in the alphabets of the first book so that they cohere with one another in such a way that, in the manner of an exhortation of supplication offered to God, they render a fitting and Latin prayer. Beneath the narrative of this prayer we can conceal whatever secrets occur to us, and write them in such a way to a friend skilled in the art that, if all the men of the whole world came together as one, they could never discover the secret lying hidden beneath the open discourse, provided it has been rightly arranged.

XIIII. Fourteenth, it must be known that in this very invention of our polygraphy there still lie hidden certain things far better than those prescribed above, namely how you will be able to instruct someone ignorant of the Latin language in a few days, not to say hours, so that he may know how to write, read, speak, and understand Latin, not indeed all things universally, but, according to a certain mode, whatever necessary matter may occur to him in his affairs on any subject. Likewise, how you may make known to your friend far away, who knows the method of the art, all the conceptions of your mind, securely, safely, and most secretly, without written messages and without the messenger knowing them, and sometimes even without a messenger, naturally and without any superstition, danger, or fear. But reason has moved us to let these things remain buried in their hiding-places, together with the rest, because it neither pleases me nor is at all advantageous to cast precious stones, once found, away, or to accomplish difficult things without the fruit of gratitude. Finally, there lie hidden beneath these things no small mysteries of subtlety concerning medicines, herbs, plants, precious stones, rivers, the members of the human body, brute animals, saints, nations, cosmography, astronomy, the seven liberal arts, and also all things that are held in human use; concerning the universal world, both of visible and of invisible things. And, lest we be more prolix than is proper, all things that are in the world, shown beforehand through the enlargement of likeness, serve the mystical teaching of the art of polygraphy.

XV. Fifteenth, note that all these inventions which we have handed down in the Polygraphy are certain small introductions in comparison with those which still remain to be discovered and written in this same faculty; concerning these we pass over in silence, lest we seem to boast vainly. For we have given to ingenious men cause for discovering greater things than we have either written or perhaps been able to discover. Let him who can add, for the completion of this invention, the things which he knows to be useful; but let him who cannot do so not rashly judge what he does not understand.

*Explanation of those things which seemed more obscure in the second book of the Polygraphy.*

In the second book of the Polygraphy, apart from the order and sequence of the words, nothing new is handed down from the first; and therefore one and the same method of working will apply in both. All the things which we said must be observed in the first, we diligently warn must also be observed in the second. In the narrative of the prayer alone a manifest difference is seen to have arisen, since the former is composed concerning our Lord, the Son of God, but the latter is raised to his mother, the ever-untouched virgin. In the second book, however, there are three hundred and eight alphabets set before the words in order, though under different subjects. For from the beginning the narrative of the discourse is directed to that lady of ours; afterward the Lord’s Prayer is expanded under twenty-four alphabets; then the angelic salutation is appended; consequently the Salve Regina; and last, a brief prayer concerning holy Anna, mother of the Virgin Mary, mother of God. In all the books of the Polygraphy one must beware that a single word not be doubled in any alphabetical order, that is, that it not be placed twice, nor that any be omitted, because it would create great confusion for the reader. Therefore the greatest care must be taken that the copies be corrected in every respect and emended exactly, lest you, in writing, fall into error. But if, through the negligence of the scribe or the carelessness of the printer, you find some word duplicated under one alphabet, erase the last and put in its place another suitable one, which has not previously occurred in the same alphabet. Know also that these two books of our Polygraphy sufficiently serve the promised operation; through them not only may you write most safely and most securely to friends whatever you wish at any time and you will be able to announce it, but also, without any suspicion, to make one who is wholly ignorant of Latin speech, provided only that he knows how to form letters, a participant in and instructed in this art, which we believe was previously unheard of in such a manner. But because this method of writing secretly under a long narrative of open discourse contains rather few sentences of hidden intent, it will perhaps therefore be usable only in great and difficult matters, especially among those who like leisure and dislike labor. We shall therefore give, in the four following books, easier methods of writing, though indeed, when looked at, they are not wholly free from suspicion, because they elude the understanding of very many. But in the *Steganographia* the methods of writing most secretly and without any suspicion are richer, where a word does not represent a letter, but one whole word represents another whole word suited to the mystery. Let us set down an example of this matter: if you wish to warn a friend of some impending danger under such a narrative of words: Melancius, Tiberius, John, and Peter, servants of the count of [aloto], have conspired to kill you, and on the fourth day after Lawrence’s day, at night, they will surround your house; consider what you should do. Steganography thus veils this secret: I beg you, dearest friend, to send me by the bearer of the present letter ten florins as a loan, because they are very necessary to me for the construction of a certain building; I shall faithfully repay them to you. Who would seek or suspect that this secret lay hidden in the discourse written above? Let us set down the same thing also under another narrative: Lord Jesus Christ, Son of the living God, who willed to become man for us, to suffer, and to die, have mercy on me, an unworthy sinner, now and in the terrible hour of my death. Behold, now you have one hidden sentence of your heart in two narratives, veiled within the bounds of steganography, in brief but faithful discourse, by which the whole method can not even be suspected, nor, if one should seek for a hundred years, could it be found [unclear], even if all the learned came together. Finally, steganography teaches you to hide this very secret sentence of your heart not only in one or two written narratives, but in almost infinite ones, by the method already mentioned, and to announce it to whomever you wish most safely, most secretly, and most securely. All methods of this sort can also be changed by metathesis into infinite [unclear], and all things can be written and announced through them. But why the work of the *Steganographia* remains buried in silence has been said sufficiently at the beginning of our *Polygraphia*; therefore it did not have to be repeated here. Let it lie hidden, then, lest it be seen by the ungrateful.

*Obscure Explanation of the Things Contained in the Third Book of Our Polygraphia*

In the third book of the *Polygraphia* we have handed down a newly devised method of writing secretly, quite secure and safe indeed, but, to all who look at it, suspect because of the hidden secret. For no one, however learned, will discover the hidden mystery without knowledge of the tradition, since he cannot understand even the open writing. We have arranged fictitious words, signifying nothing in themselves, but representing only the letters of the alphabet set before them, through which you may write and make known all the secrets of your mind to a friend most securely, however absent he may be. But the unskilled, ignorant of the art, when they have seen the writing, will think it is some foreign language; they will be wholly ignorant of what it contains. But when you wish to write or announce something to an absent person by the method of this book, take care first of all that you distinguish every single word properly from the others, so that each one can be very clearly discerned from another, lest the person to whom you write take one for two, or two for one, because that would give birth to a great error. Let us set down an example of this matter.

Abrin madon badis, cadulur pasu, adin loroff masara damis, boda omodromi drus uares mastron pha lares medas rassut mandebor tara, mesen baiu phosat, galos, prosol fane lemoros, aroso cabilneso, hamol heliet rhamil fesia doron hubur rodex. In this narrative of fictitious speech is contained this brief sentence of a secret: a friend warning a friend about danger. Do not come to court, because the king is angry with you. You see how easy, safe, and secure this method is for writing and announcing whatever you wish to a friend who understands it. In all cases, however, you should know that care must be taken, as was said before, that you do not take more than one word from any alphabet, nor again skip over any alphabet. As often as a narrative

[unclear] of the contrived discourse to the end of the book, before the secret of your mind has yet been completed, you should begin again from the beginning, and do this as often as necessary. Nevertheless anyone may, at his own discretion, change these words of ours, improve them, and add more, provided however that he avoids a consonance breaking out awkwardly into words.

*Explanation of the fourth book of our Polygraphia.*

[unclear]

In the fourth book of the Polygraphia we have in like manner placed, in alphabetical order, words contrived by us, but in one point alone we deliberately differ from the preceding book. For in the preceding book the words of their own proper force contain nothing other than that they represent the letters set before them at the head. But in the fourth book, which we now have in hand, each word contains its own proper force, and nevertheless designates the letters placed before it. They contain within what they display without, because the second letter of each word always serves the hidden mystery, as we shall make clear by the following example: Ecorach banasar dulay semalas, chorion suriel anephor schabaras, busarat vimalo tumelech amasar. To understand the mystery of this, take only the second letter from each word; you will find this hidden sense: “Beware this man.” For the second letter in Ecorach is c, in banasar a, in dulay u, in semalas e. Join these four letters and they will make this word: “Beware.” And so one proceeds with the rest up to the end of the book; if anything of the hidden mystery remains to be written, one must return to the beginning as many times as necessary, until the secret has been wholly completed. In this very secret mode of writing, if someone has been trained by long practice, he has no need of a book, but will readily compose ex tempore words suited to his purpose. But someone less practiced in composing must have a copy of the book from which to write, since without the book he may easily find the hidden secret everywhere by means of the second letters. Yet in great, difficult, or dangerous matters, this method of writing is not to be trusted quite safely, because the industry of a skilled man could, by chance, discover the hidden mystery. This method of writing can also be altered by an intelligent man, so that the significant letters are placed in the third or fourth position of the word, with the words altered.

*Brief Explanation of the Fifth Book of our Polygraphia.*

In the fifth book of the Polygraphia we have transmitted various modes of writing secretly by transposition of ordinary letters, arranged in such coordination that under their veil we shall be able safely to write and announce everything to an absent friend. Now, to speak in our own manner, the progression of the transposition of letters is almost infinite, since from one alphabet innumerable alphabets arise by metathesis. First we described a straight table containing twenty-four alphabets; by understanding it, as many alphabets can be composed as there are stars counted in the firmament of heaven. For as many characters as there are in that table, so many times, by art and in order, do ten hundred thousand alphabets arise. After this we distributed an inverted table, which arises into another as often as you change the first letter at the head. The first letter in the straight table is b, in the inverted table z. Whenever you put any other letter in their place, you will find the table varied and entirely new in all respects, and so on to infinity. Then we expanded the first straight table, placing over the head of each black letter, in red, the letter which it represents, so that we might provide the reader with an easier mode of writing. This is the mode of writing: in the first black alphabet, take one letter of the hidden sentence; from the second, another; from the third, a third; and so consequently up to the end. When you have reached it, remember to return as often as necessary to the first order, until by concealing it you have completed the secret mystery of your mind. But so that you may see the order, let us give an example: Hxpf gfbmcʒ fueib gmbt ege rbd qopma uwu.wf xegk ak.tnr qxyx. The meaning of this mystical discourse

[unclear] is. Beware this man, because he is evil, a thief, a deceiver, a liar, and unjust. You now see, reader, what a remarkable transposition of the letters of the alphabet this table produces, since there is no one who, without knowledge of it, can penetrate this secret. For this method of writing exceeds every common transposition of letters, since each individual letter is always changed from one series of the alphabet into another. From the reversed table also, which we have expanded in order with a similar arrangement, let us set down this example by way of introduction: Rdkt, stznyb, tevq[unclear], snzf, sdrgh, vfd. Its secret meaning is this: Beware this man, because he is evil. And note that under the example of the right-hand table already set down, we led the hidden series from the beginning through the whole of it, and then, continuing in like manner through the reversed table, we again make a circle, as you see, to the beginning of the right-hand table. Hence it must be considered that one series of writing can be led through both tables, as we have already done in the demonstration of the first example, or, if you prefer, through one alone, as we have sufficiently shown in the reversed table. Nor will it cause difficulty for the reader to whom you send the hidden message; he will easily understand everything if he has had even a little practice in the art. But the strongest lesson, which increases knowledge, is frequent use in any art.

After the expansion of the tables there follow certain *orchemata*, in which there is not only a transposition of all the letters, but also an ordered leaping of one over another. For *orchema* is called *transilitio*, “leaping across,” in Latin. And first we marked the leap of the letters in expanded form; afterward we reduced it into an *orchema*. In the operation of this *orchema* there is a remarkable leap. There are twenty-four alphabets in it. In the first no letter intervenes; in the second, one; in the third, two; in the fifth, three; in the sixth, five; in the seventh, six; in the eighth, seven; in the ninth, eight; in the tenth, nine; finally in the eleventh, ten, and so on successively as far as the twenty-fourth alphabet, in which twenty-three letters intervene. Afterward there is a return by likeness to the beginning. The method in the *orchemata* for the mystery of writing is similar to that which is observed [unclear] of the *orchema*: from any black alphabet take only one letter, which has been shown written in red at the head. For understanding this, let us set down an example: Prdb. ifyqyn. bdyen. zbkt. sxfol. inq. Its sentence is this: Beware this man, because he is evil. When, in writing, you have reached the end of the *orchemata* and the secret begun in your heart has not yet been completed, you should know that you must return to the beginning as often as necessary, until the mystery has been completed through all things. And know that from one *orchema* of letters as many alphabets are composed as the diligent investigator may wish to have. Finally, this method of writing by *orchemata*, that is, by transilitions and leaps, is the most secure of all, because the transposition of letters arises not in one uniform way but varied in many ways. And it should be noted that true and perfect knowledge of the tables and *orchemata*, if you are industrious and studious, will give you understanding of all writings that are written in any way by transposition of letters. And it is a great secret, known to few, that all meanings hidden in any transposition of letters can be found.

*Brief Explanation of the Sixth and Last Book of the Polygraphy*

In the sixth and last book of the *Polygraphy* we have brought together certain characters of the ancients which were used in place of letters, so that, if anyone wishes to appropriate his own alphabet from it, he may be able to do so, although anyone moderately skilled, if he wishes, can devise for himself characters more beautiful than these. All the systems of this book are sufficiently open and clear, and therefore they need no explanation, even a brief one. Yet those which have been newly invented by us require a definite declaration, lest perhaps they seem to have been set down without reason. First of all, we have made all the letters of the Latins numerical, after the manner of the Hebrews and Greeks, thinking it shameful for us if we should be found inferior to them in this respect. For what is it that we have doubled certain letters so that they might suffice for an infinite number, when the Jews long ago did the very same thing, compelled by the same necessity? And the Greeks, because of the lack of letters, admitted certain marks into their number, since they were unable to double the letters. But we, because we are pressed by an equal lack of letters, have admitted two marks, namely this one and the one which our writers put for *et*; nor does it matter if it is written thus, *et*, provided that it represents six in number. The other, however, we have added, [unclear], which our writers, in abbreviating They write for *con* and *com* indifferently, and here in the order of numbers it represents six hundred. We have also, by the same necessity, doubled two letters, namely *r, r* and *s, s*; of these, *r* signifies ninety, but curved *r* one hundred; long *s* two hundred, and closed *s* three hundred. Further, *w*, born as a double letter, which because of the nature of the German language we have placed in the order of the alphabet, represents the last letter, and in the series of numbers one thousand. Let us set down in a table an example of the numerical method of this new invention of ours, so that anyone who wishes to know the method may have a sure instruction before his eyes. For what has always been easy for both the Hebrews and the Greeks will not be impossible for us. The table of numbers follows.

[unclear table of numbers] We have sufficiently shown the method of numbering by Latin letters after the manner of the Hebrews and the Greeks; we have reasonably placed it here by way of digression, although it is also found earlier in the sixth book. But by the fact that, as we proceeded, we have here enclosed our numbering in thirty times one hundred thousand, we plainly declare that it can be extended to infinity. If this method of numbers displeases anyone, he will easily be able to devise a better one. Also in imitation of the Normans, from the numerical letters written above, we have arranged a new alphabet, by which you will be able safely to write and announce to an absent friend any secret whatsoever that you wish. This method of writing is so secret, because it is both new and before us was never seen or heard, that it seems to me wholly impossible for it to be understood by anyone without the service of a teacher. But now let us set down its alphabet.

[unclear alphabet table] C - ij Behold, you now have here this alphabet made from Roman numeral letters in imitation of the [Normans?], with which you will be able to write and report very safely to an absent friend whatever you wish; and no one lying in wait will be able to penetrate your secret except him who knows the hidden principle of the art. For all who see the writing will think that the letters have been transposed, and they will labor in vain in reckoning them up. Iaaibid ifeib, ieifeigeicbkib. cakeibid. ikabk. gidibid. eigih. iceigigh ibkig. kif. iaaihifid, ibeicdakaieb. icei kaib. The hidden meaning of this writing is as follows: Beware the bearer of the present letters, because he is a very wicked man, a thief, a robber, a liar, and worthless. From the demonstration of this example it is easy to understand how safe and secure this method of writing is universally, in every language known to the writer, and in every matter or business, however difficult, secret, great, or lengthy. Nor do I think that there lives in the world a man who, by his own wit and without a teacher, could read this writing itself. For all who see it will think it is nothing other than transposed letters; and when they have been unable to obtain an understanding of them, at last worn out by despair, they will cease from investigation, and whatever you have written will remain hidden. For no one, as I think, will find out this craft of writing secretly by his own powers, as it is in itself, unless he either has the Polygraphy, or this key to it, at hand, or has been taught by another. From these numeral letters also many other alphabets can be made according to the order of numbering, when the beginning for writing alphabetically is taken not from unity but from any other following number. But in that progression you should know that care must be taken that no more than two numerable signs are taken to represent one letter. Hence, when you have come to ww, that is, ten times one hundred thousand, stop your step and do not go further, lest the multitude of signs, by deforming it, confuse the beauty of the writing. For, after unity has been set aside, ten times one hundred thousand signs included are sufficient for almost thirty individual alphabets. Yet the method of the first alphabet, beginning with unity, is the more elegant for enveloping written secrets, inasmuch as it is shorter and simpler. Finally, it makes the writing less tortuous. We have also arranged another alphabet with Latin letters distributed in confused order and transposed away from [unclear] rule, in imitation of which others too can be made almost without number. Its likeness is now set down.

| o | a | t | g | c | n | 9 | t |
| p | b | b | a | k | u |
| q | c | u | i | b | n | x |
| r | d | w | k | y | m | y |
| i | e | x | l | d | l | z̃ |
| s | f | õ | m | g | f | w | Let us set down an example of this alphabet, so that we may prove that a secret can lie hidden in it. Gquog, ozuqi, ykar, din, uc, qacquxua, ríqdikuꝯ 9i, aqqurixrk3, skti, idta, ykoc9aqukg, uc, coddotacuoʒ, osoqui, iu kg, ci, bidiog. This writing, however, contains this meaning within itself: Know, friend, that the king has decreed in council that you are to be killed. Flee therefore as quickly as possible into [Aragon?] from his presence, lest you perish. This method of writing too is tortuous; it is subject to no regular rule, since it can be varied and changed at the pleasure of the writer by a chance transposition as much as anyone wishes. Next it should be noted that at the end of the sixth book of the Polygraphy we have handed down another method of writing by means of numeral signs, which people commonly call ciphers; under its covering you will safely write to an absent friend whatever you wish, and no one ignorant of the art will be able either to discover or to suspect that a secret lies hidden there, especially if the rules that we have given on these matters are observed with diligent caution. This is that the sheet should be formed in the manner of an astronomical table, as though it contained something pertaining to the reckoning of the stars, namely so that characters of the signs or planets are inserted, so that someone into whose hands it may perhaps fall by chance, led on by the likeness of a table, may think that no secret lies hidden in it, but may believe that everything pertains to some astronomical calculation. We have arranged twelve alphabets according to the number of the twelve signs of the zodiac, assigning to each one the number twenty-four in place of letters; the first of these, of Aries, begins from unity and ends at twenty-four. We advise in important matters C iiij

[In great?] and difficult matters one must be on guard against uniformity, because this mode of writing, on account of that very thing, can easily be suspect. But whoever wishes prudently to use his natural industry will find subtler things from ours, and what we have handed down in a not sufficiently polished form he will easily make better. In the numerical orchema great secrets lie hidden; if anyone has properly understood it, he will penetrate many mysteries of numbers. For we had neither time nor reason on this occasion to publish everything, but in our judgment what we have done will be enough for the instruction of those to whom our discoveries will someday be pleasing, so that they may have many methods not only of writing secretly whatever they wish to absent partners in the art, but also one method of extending it into many better and subtler ones, according to each person's capacity of understanding. Likewise, since the numerical orchemata have been made sufficiently open and clear in their places, and at the same time their tables set forth, it was not necessary here to repeat things once said, lest excessive length produce weariness for the reader. But I did not think it necessary to set down an example of writing more secretly by means of numerical figures, since there is no difficulty in the method of knowing it for all who possess even moderate ability. With similar ease the very ancient signs of numbering that we have brought back to light can be understood and grasped; by them the knowledge of numbers is carried on to infinity. For whenever a stroke is placed above a smaller number, a thousand is indicated. Thus X [with a stroke] means ten thousand; C [with a stroke], one hundred thousand; M [with a stroke], a thousand thousand; XL [with a stroke], ten times a thousand thousand; CC [with a stroke], a hundred times a thousand thousand; MM [with a stroke] means a thousand times a thousand thousand, and so consequently as far and as much as you wish to advance in numbering, without limit.

*Here ends the Key of the Polygraphia of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of [Paeapolis?], to Emperor Maximilian. Printed under the direction of Johann Haselberg of Aia, bookseller, in the year from the birth of Christ 1518, in the month of July.*
